   200	    def attr_path(self, p):
   201	        if len(p) == 1:
   202	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], None, None)
   203	
   204	        elif len(p) == 2 and isinstance(p[0], ast.Filter) and isinstance(p[1], ast.SubAttr):

   206	            if sub_attr is not None:
   207	                raise SCIMParserError(f'Parsing error at: {p}')
   208	
   209	            # For easier transpiling, convert complex queries like so:
   210	            # emails[type eq "Primary"].value -> emails.value[type eq "Primary"]
   211	            p[0].namespace.sub_attr = p[1]
   212	
   213	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], None, None)
   214

   217

   219	            return ast.AttrPath(p[1], None, p[0])
   220	
   221	        else:
   222	            return ast.AttrPath(p[1], p[2], p[0])

That's a neat argument. So, if there existed a function y in Y such that ||x - y|| < 0.5, then integrating the inequality x - y(x) < 0.5 and y(x) - x < 0.5 over [0,1] would lead to a contradiction. Wait, but in the previous step, we only used one side of the inequality. Let me check again.

If |x - y(x)| < 0.5 for all x, then y(x) > x - 0.5 and y(x) < x + 0.5. Then integrating y(x) over [0,1] would give ∫ y(x) dx > ∫ (x - 0.5) dx and ∫ y(x) dx < ∫ (x + 0.5) dx. However, since ∫ y(x) dx = 0, then 0 > ∫ (x - 0.5) dx = 0 and 0 < ∫ (x + 0.5) dx = 0.5 + 0.5 = 1. But ∫ (x - 0.5) dx is indeed 0.5 - 0.5 = 0, so 0 > 0 is a contradiction. Therefore, the lower bound gives a contradiction, but the upper bound is okay. Therefore, this shows that there is no function y ∈ Y such that ||x - y|| < 0.5, so the distance must be at least 0.5.

Now, to show that the distance is exactly 0.5, we need to find a sequence of functions y_n in Y such that ||x - y_n|| approaches 0.5. Let's try to construct such functions.

One approach is to consider functions y_n that are equal to x - 0.5 except near the endpoints, adjusted to satisfy y_n(0) = 0 and continuity. Wait, if we take y(x) = x - 0.5, then y(0) = -0.5, which is not in X. So, we need to modify this function near x=0 to make y(0)=0 while keeping the integral zero.

Alternatively, consider functions that are equal to x - 0.5 + c, where c is a constant. Wait, if we add a constant to y(x), then the integral would change. Wait, but if we set y(x) = x - 0.5 + c, then ∫₀¹ y(x) dx = ∫ (x - 0.5 + c) dx = 0.5 - 0.5 + c = c. To have ∫ y(x) dx = 0, we need c = 0. So y(x) = x - 0.5, but as mentioned, this function has y(0) = -0.5, which is not allowed. So, we need to adjust it near zero.

So, let's modify y(x) = x - 0.5 in a neighborhood around x=0 to make y(0) = 0. Let's define a function y_n(x) such that:

- For x ∈ [1/n, 1], y_n(x) = x - 0.5
- For x ∈ [0, 1/n], y_n(x) is a linear function connecting (0,0) to (1/n, 1/n - 0.5)

This way, y_n is continuous, satisfies y_n(0) = 0, and is equal to x - 0.5 on [1/n,1]. Let's check the integral of y_n over [0,1].

The integral will be the integral over [0,1/n] of the linear part plus the integral over [1/n,1] of x - 0.5.

First, let's compute the linear part. The linear function from (0,0) to (1/n, 1/n - 0.5). The slope m is (1/n - 0.5 - 0)/(1/n - 0) = (1/n - 0.5)/(1/n) = 1 - 0.5n.

Therefore, the linear part is y_n(x) = (1 - 0.5n)x for x ∈ [0,1/n].

So, the integral over [0,1/n] is ∫₀^{1/n} (1 - 0.5n)x dx = (1 - 0.5n) * [x²/2]₀^{1/n} = (1 - 0.5n) * (1/(2n²)).

The integral over [1/n,1] is ∫_{1/n}^1 (x - 0.5) dx = [x²/2 - 0.5x]_{1/n}^1 = (1/2 - 0.5) - [(1/(2n²) - 0.5/n)] = (0 - 0) - [1/(2n²) - 0.5/n] = -1/(2n²) + 0.5/n.

Therefore, total integral is (1 - 0.5n)/(2n²) - 1/(2n²) + 0.5/n = [ (1 - 0.5n) - 1 ] / (2n²) + 0.5/n = (-0.5n)/(2n²) + 0.5/n = (-0.25)/n + 0.5/n = (0.5 - 0.25)/n = 0.25/n.

Wait, but we need the integral of y_n over [0,1] to be zero. However, in this case, it's 0.25/n. So, this is not zero. Therefore, our construction is flawed.

Hmm, so we need to adjust the function such that the integral over [0,1] is zero. Let's see. Let's instead of just connecting (0,0) to (1/n, 1/n - 0.5), perhaps we can adjust the linear part so that the total integral becomes zero.

Let me denote the linear part as y_n(x) = ax for x ∈ [0,1/n], and y_n(x) = x - 0.5 + b for x ∈ [1/n,1]. Then, we need to choose a and b such that:

1. The function is continuous at x=1/n: a*(1/n) = (1/n) - 0.5 + b.
2. The integral over [0,1] is zero: ∫₀^{1/n} ax dx + ∫_{1/n}^1 (x - 0.5 + b) dx = 0.

Let's solve these equations.

First, continuity at x=1/n:

a*(1/n) = (1/n) - 0.5 + b => b = a*(1/n) - (1/n) + 0.5.

Second, the integral:

∫₀^{1/n} ax dx = a*(1/(2n²)).

∫_{1/n}^1 (x - 0.5 + b) dx = ∫_{1/n}^1 x dx - 0.5 ∫_{1/n}^1 dx + b ∫_{1/n}^1 dx

Compute each part:

∫_{1/n}^1 x dx = [x²/2]_{1/n}^1 = 1/2 - 1/(2n²)

-0.5 ∫_{1/n}^1 dx = -0.5*(1 - 1/n) = -0.5 + 0.5/n

b ∫_{1/n}^1 dx = b*(1 - 1/n)

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Understanding and Planning the Fix



tool: Your thought has been logged.

assistant: ### 5.1 Problem Statement
The issue is that the SCIM filter parser cannot handle queries with three or more consecutive "or" or "and" operators because these operators are defined as 'nonassoc' (non-associative) in the precedence rules.

### 5.2 Problem Location
The problem is located in `/testbed/src/scim2_filter_parser/parser.py` at lines 112-116 where the precedence is defined.

### 5.3 Test Reproduction
My reproduction script confirms that:
- Queries with 3+ consecutive same operators fail: `A or B or C` and `A and B and C`
- Queries with 2 operators work fine: `A or B` and `A and B`  
- Mixed operator queries work: `A and B or C`

### 5.4 Best Practices
- Follow standard operator precedence: NOT > AND > OR
- Make operators left-associative for natural left-to-right evaluation
- Ensure backward compatibility with existing working queries

### 5.5 Fix Implementation
Change the precedence rules from 'nonassoc' to proper associativity:
- OR: left-associative (lowest precedence)  
- AND: left-associative (higher than OR)
- NOT: right-associative (highest precedence)

Thus， the entire Gray code sequence forms a cycle, and so the sequence can be rotated to start anywhere, and still have adjacent nodes differing by one bit.

 Thus the solution is possible regardless of the starting point. Hence, the rotated list is a valid path.

 Thus the algorithm is:

For each test case:

1. Check if D <4 → then output -2.

2. Else, proceed:

   a. Convert the starting point S into a binary string (each coordinate is -1 converted to 0, 1 to1).

   b. Find the index k where this binary string's Gray code value equals the corresponding Gray code number for the all-0 starting?

Wait No: need the S's binary converted value (as an integer), call this "start_num".

We compute its position in the Gray code sequence.

 To compute which position in the standard Gray code sequence corresponds to the 'start_num' .

But how?

Wait the gray code sequence for N is such that the m-th element of the linear sequence corresponds to the value G(m) = m ^ (m >>1). Thus the inverse: given a desired gray code value g (which corresponds to the Start's mapped value), find m such that G(m) =g.

Once we have that m, the desired starting element will be in the m-th position of the sequence.

 Once found, we can rotate the sequence to start at that position, giving a valid path.

For example, take y_n(x) = x - 0.5 + c_n, where c_n is adjusted so that y_n(0) = 0. Wait, but y_n(0) = 0 -0.5 + c_n = c_n -0.5 =0 => c_n =0.5. Then y_n(x) =x -0.5 +0.5 =x. So that approach doesn't work.

Alternatively, let's take a function y_n which is equal to x -0.5 except in a small interval [0, δ_n], where δ_n is chosen such that the integral over [0,1] is zero. Let's define δ_n such that:

The integral of y_n over [0,1] is zero. Let's suppose y_n(x) = x -0.5 for x ∈ [δ_n,1], and y_n(x) is some function on [0, δ_n] that connects 0 to δ_n -0.5 smoothly.

But again, the integral would be the integral over [0, δ_n] of y_n(x) plus the integral over [δ_n,1] of x -0.5.

Let’s denote the integral over [0, δ_n] as I1 and over [δ_n,1] as I2.

I2 = ∫_{δ_n}^1 (x -0.5)dx = [0.5x² -0.5x]_{δ_n}^1 = (0.5 -0.5) - (0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n) = -0.5δ_n² +0.5δ_n

To make the total integral zero, I1 + I2 =0 => I1 = -I2 = 0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n

Now, we need to define y_n on [0, δ_n] such that it connects 0 to y_n(δ_n) = δ_n -0.5, and the integral over [0, δ_n] is 0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n. Let’s choose a linear function for simplicity.

Suppose y_n(x) = ax + b on [0, δ_n]. Since y_n(0) =0, b=0. Therefore, y_n(x) = ax. At x=δ_n, we have y_n(δ_n) = aδ_n = δ_n -0.5. Therefore, a =1 -0.5/δ_n.

But the integral over [0, δ_n] is ∫₀^{δ_n} ax dx = 0.5 a δ_n². Set this equal to I1=0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n.

Thus:

0.5 a δ_n² =0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n

Multiply both sides by 2:

a δ_n² = δ_n² - δ_n

But a =1 -0.5/δ_n, so:

(1 -0.5/δ_n) δ_n² = δ_n² - δ_n

Left side: δ_n² -0.5 δ_n

Right side: δ_n² - δ_n

Thus:

δ_n² -0.5 δ_n = δ_n² - δ_n

Subtract δ_n² from both sides:

-0.5 δ_n = - δ_n

Add δ_n to both sides:

0.5 δ_n =0 => δ_n=0

But δ_n cannot be zero. Therefore, there's no solution unless δ_n=0, which is trivial. Therefore, a linear function on [0, δ_n] cannot satisfy the integral condition unless δ_n=0. Therefore, we need a different function on [0, δ_n].

Maybe a quadratic function? Let’s try.

Let’s define y_n(x) on [0, δ_n] as a quadratic function such that y_n(0)=0, y_n(δ_n)=δ_n -0.5, and the integral over [0, δ_n] is 0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n.

Let’s suppose y_n(x)= ax² +bx +c. Since y_n(0)=0, c=0.

Thus, y_n(x)=ax² +bx. At x=δ_n, y_n(δ_n)=aδ_n² +bδ_n=δ_n -0.5.

Integral over [0, δ_n]: ∫₀^{δ_n} (ax² +bx)dx = (a/3)δ_n³ + (b/2)δ_n² =0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n.

Therefore, we have the system:

1. aδ_n² +bδ_n=δ_n -0.5

2. (a/3)δ_n³ + (b/2)δ_n²=0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n

Let’s solve this system for a and b.

From equation 1:

aδ_n² +bδ_n=δ_n -0.5 => aδ_n +b=1 -0.5/δ_n (divided both sides by δ_n)

From equation 2:

(a/3)δ_n³ + (b/2)δ_n²=0.5δ_n² -0.5δ_n

Divide both sides by δ_n²:

(a/3)δ_n + (b/2)=0.5 -0.5/δ_n

Now, we have:

From equation 1: aδ_n +b=1 -0.5/δ_n

From equation 2: (a/3)δ_n + (b/2)=0.5 -0.5/δ_n

Let’s denote equation 1 as:

Equation 1: aδ_n +b=1 -0.5/δ_n

Equation 2: (1/3)aδ_n + (1/2)b=0.5 -0.5/δ_n

Let’s solve this system. Let’s denote u = aδ_n and v = b.

Then equation 1: u + v =1 -0.5/δ_n

Equation 2: (1/3)u + (1/2)v =0.5 -0.5/δ_n

Multiply equation 2 by 6 to eliminate denominators:

2u +3v =3 -3/δ_n

From equation 1: u =1 -0.5/δ_n -v

Substitute into equation 2:

2(1 -0.5/δ_n -v) +3v=3 -3/δ_n

Expand:

2 -1/δ_n -2v +3v=3 -3/δ_n

Simplify:

2 -1/δ_n +v=3 -3/δ_n

Therefore:

v=3 -3/δ_n -2 +1/δ_n=1 -2/δ_n

Thus, v=1 -2/δ_n

From equation 1: u +v=1 -0.5/δ_n => u=1 -0.5/δ_n -v=1 -0.5/δ_n -1 +2/δ_n=1.5/δ_n

So, u=1.5/δ_n

Recall that u=aδ_n, so a=u/δ_n=1.5/δ_n²

Similarly, v=b=1 -2/δ_n

Therefore, the quadratic function is:

y_n(x)= (1.5/δ_n²)x² + (1 -2/δ_n)x

But we need y_n(x) to be continuous at x=δ_n. Wait, we already ensured that by construction. Let's check the value at x=δ_n:

y_n(δ_n)= (1.5/δ_n²)(δ_n²) + (1 -2/δ_n)δ_n=1.5 + δ_n -2= δ_n -0.5

Which matches the required value. Good.

But what is the<#sep#> code sequence.

 To compute which position in the standard Gray code sequence corresponds to the 'start_num' .

But how?

Wait the gray code sequence for N is such that the m-th element of the linear sequence corresponds to the value G(m) = m ^ (m >>1). Thus the inverse: given a desired gray code value g (which corresponds to the Start's mapped value), find m such that G(m) =g.

Once we have that m, the desired starting element will be in the m-th position of the sequence.

 Once found, we can rotate the sequence to start at that position, giving a valid path.

 Hence, the algorithm steps are:

- For the S's coordinates:

   Convert to bits (0→-1, 1→1.

   Make an integer number (as the binary of these bits).

   call this number as 'start_num'.

- Generate the entire list of gray codes for N bits:

   list_of_gray = [gray[m] = m ^ (m >>1) for m in 0..2^N-1 ]

- Find the index (m) where gray[m] equals start_num. 

- rotate the list so the starting element is first. 

- Iterate through the rotated list and convert each to coordinates.

- output each as the coordinates.

But how to compute m for a give start_num?

That requires solving m such that m ^ (m >>1) = g (the target gray code value).

 This inversion is non-trivial.

 Alternatively, perhaps, iterate through all possible m from 0 to 2^N-1 and check which m gives the desired gray code value.

 For N up to say, 20, this might be feasible? 2^20 is about a million which is manageable.

Wait in practice, for code-writing purposes, this might be manageable.

 For N up to 20, 2^20 is about a million iterations, but for even N=30 it would be impossible. But likely the problem expects N to be small enough for this approach.

 Hence, the steps:

To implement this:

We can loop through each m from 0 to 2^N-1 and compute m ^ (m >>1 ), find the m where this equals the desired gray code value (代表 S的 bits' value).

 Once found.

 then the new starting point's index is m.

 the list of the gray codes is deemed a cycle, so we can make a list like:

 rotated_gray = gray[m:] + gray[:m]

 this creates a new list starting with the m-th element first.

 then we iterate through this rotated list, and for each element in the list, we convert the gray code value back to coordinates.

The M^th element in this rotated list will be the next start, and so on.

Therefore, the quadratic function is:

y_n(x)= (1.5/δ_n²)x² + (1 -2/δ_n)x

But we need y_n(x) to be continuous at x=δ_n. Wait, we already ensured that by construction. Let's check the value at x=δ_n:

y_n(δ_n)= (1.5/δ_n²)(δ_n²) + (1 -2/δ_n)δ_n=1.5 + δ_n -2= δ_n -0.5

Which matches the required value. Good.

But what is the behavior of this function? Let's analyze for δ_n approaching zero.

Suppose we take δ_n approaching zero. Then, the coefficients become:

a=1.5/δ_n² → ∞

b=1 -2/δ_n → -∞

But the function y_n(x) on [0, δ_n] is a quadratic with very large coefficients. However, perhaps the maximum difference |x - y_n(x)| on [0, δ_n] can be controlled.

Let’s compute the difference x - y_n(x) on [0, δ_n]:

x - y_n(x)=x - [1.5/δ_n² x² + (1 -2/δ_n)x] =x -1.5/δ_n² x² -x +2/δ_n x= -1.5/δ_n² x² +2/δ_n x= x( -1.5x/δ_n² +2/δ_n )

Let’s factor out x:

=x( -1.5x/δ_n² +2/δ_n )

We can write this as:

x*(2/δ_n -1.5x/δ_n² )=x/δ_n*(2 -1.5x/δ_n )

Let’s set t =x/δ_n ∈ [0,1]

Then, the expression becomes:

x/δ_n*(2 -1.5t )=t*(2 -1.5t )

Therefore, the difference x - y_n(x)= δ_n t*(2 -1.5t ) for t ∈ [0,1]

This is a quadratic function in t. Let's find its maximum on [0,1].

The function f(t)=t*(2 -1.5t)=2t -1.5t². Its derivative is 2 -3t, which is zero at t=2/3. Therefore, maximum at t=2/3:

f(2/3)=2*(2/3) -1.5*(4/9)=4/3 - 6/9=4/3 -2/3=2/3

Therefore, maximum difference on [0, δ_n] is δ_n*(2/3)= (2/3)δ_n

On [δ_n,1], the difference is x - y_n(x)=x - (x -0.5)=0.5

Therefore, the maximum difference overall is the maximum between (2/3)δ_n and 0.5. As δ_n approaches zero, (2/3)δ_n approaches zero, so the maximum difference approaches 0.5. Therefore, for the constructed y_n, ||x - y_n||=max{0.5, (2/3)δ_n}=0.5 when δ_n is sufficiently small. Wait, but when δ_n is small, (2/3)δ_n is less than 0.5, so the maximum difference is 0.5.

Wait, no. On [δ_n,1], the difference is 0.5, and on [0,δ_n], the difference is up to (2/3)δ_n. So, if (2/3)δ_n ≤0.5, then the overall maximum is 0.5. But as δ_n approaches zero, (2/3)δ_n approaches zero, so the maximum difference is indeed 0.5. Therefore, for any δ_n >0, ||x - y_n||=0.5. Wait, but that can't be, because when δ_n approaches zero, the function y_n is equal to x -0.5 on most of the interval, except near zero, where it dips down to zero. But the maximum difference would be 0.5, achieved on [δ_n,1]. However, we need to check continuity.

Wait, at x=δ_n, y_n(x)=x -0.5, so the difference is 0.5, which is the same as on [δ_n,1]. So, the maximum difference is indeed 0.5 across the entire interval [0,1], except near zero, where the difference is smaller. Therefore, the norm ||x - y_n||=0.5 for all n. But wait, that would mean that the distance is exactly 0.5, achieved by these functions. However, when δ_n approaches zero, the function y_n approaches x -0.5, which is not in Y because it's discontinuous at zero. But each y_n is continuous, satisfies y_n(0)=0, and ∫ y_n=0. Therefore, each y_n is in Y, and ||x - y_n||=0.5. Therefore, the distance is exactly 0.5, achieved as a limit by these functions.

Wait, but if each y_n has ||x - y_n||=0.5, then the infimum is 0.5, and since Y is closed, does that mean that there is a function y in Y such that ||x - y||=0.5? Or is the infimum not achieved?

In this case, the limit of the functions y_n as δ_n approaches zero would be x -0.5, which is not in Y because it's not continuous at zero (it has a jump from -0.5 to 0 at zero). But since each y_n is continuous and in Y, and the distance is always 0.5, then the infimum is 0.5, but it's not achieved by any function in Y. Hence, the distance is 0.5, but there is no closest function in Y to x. This is similar to how in some spaces, the infimum is not achieved.

Therefore, combining both arguments: the lower bound of 0.5 via the integral argument, and the upper bound of 0.5 via the sequence y_n, we conclude that the distance is indeed 0.5.

Therefore, the answer is 0.5, and the verification is complete.

**Final Answer**
The distance from the function \( f = x \) to the set \( Y \) is \(\boxed{0.5}\).

To verify that the distance from the function \( f(x) = x \) to the set \( Y = \{ f \in C[0,1] : \int_0^1 f = 0 \} \) is 0.5, we can use both analytical arguments and construction of specific functions.

1. **Lower Bound Argument**:
   - Suppose there exists a function \( y \in Y \) such that \( \|x - y\| < 0.5 \). This implies \( |x - y(x)| < 0.5 \) for all \( x \in [0,1] \).
   - Integrating both sides over \([0,1]\), we get:
     \[
     \int_0^1 (x - y(x)) \, dx < \int_0^1 0.5 \, dx
     \]
   - Since \( \int_0^1 y(x) \, dx = 0 \), the left-hand side becomes \( \int_0^1 x \, dx = 0.5 \). However, the right-hand side is \( 0.5 \), leading to a contradiction. Thus, the distance must be at least 0.5.

2. **Upper Bound Construction**:
   - Construct a sequence of functions \( y_n \in Y \) such that \( \|x - y_n\| \) approaches 0.5.

## Phase 1. READING: Understanding the Problem



tool: Your thought has been logged.

assistant: ### 1.1-1.3 Problem Analysis

The issue is with the SCIM (System for Cross-domain Identity Management) filter parser. The parser fails when processing queries with multiple consecutive identical logical operators ("or" or "and"), but works fine with mixed operators.

**Key technical details:**
- Error message: `scim2_filter_parser.parser.SCIMParserError: Parsing error at: Token(type='OR', value='or', lineno=1, index=52)`
- Failing queries contain multiple same operators (3 "or" or 3 "and")
- Working query contains mixed operators (1 "and" + 1 "or")

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Parse a query with 3 consecutive "or" operators: `"displayName co \"username\" or nickName co \"username\" or userName co \"username\""`
2. Parse a query with 3 consecutive "and" operators: `"displayName co \"username\" and nickName co \"username\" and userName co \"username\""`
3. Both should fail with parsing error
4. Parse a mixed query: `"displayName co \"username\" and nickName co \"username\" or userName co \"username\""` - this should work

### 1.5 Best Practices
- Ensure left-associativity for logical operators
- Maintain proper operator precedence (AND typically has higher precedence than OR)
- Test edge cases with multiple operators
- Preserve backward compatibility

Update: this is now on the OEIS as A309159: Number of generalized polyforms on the snub square tiling with n cells.
Definitions
The snub square tiling is a semiregular tiling of the plane that consists of equilateral triangles and squares.

A pseudo-polyform on the snub square tiling is a plane figure constructed by joining together these triangles and squares along their shared sides, analogous to a polyomino. Here is an example of a six-cell and an eight-cell pseudo-polyform:

Examples
For n = 1 there are two 1-cell pseudo-polyforms, namely the square and the triangle:

For n = 2 there are two 2-cell pseudo-polyforms, namely a square with a triangle and two triangles.

For n = 3 there are four 3-cell pseudo-polyforms.

Challenge
The goal of this challenge is to compute as many terms as possible in this sequence, which begins 2, 2, 4, ... and where the n-th term is the number of n-cell pseudo-polyforms up to rotation and reflection.
Run your code for as long as you'd like. The winner of this challenge will be the user who posts the most terms of the sequence, along with their code. If two users post the same number of terms, then whoever posts their last term earliest wins.
(Once there are enough known terms to prove that this sequence does not already exist in the OEIS, I'll create an entry in the OEIS and list the contributor as a co-author if he or she desires.)

   138	``scim2_filter_parser.queries.SQLQuery`` class with an attribute map and optionally
   139	any joins necessary to make all required fields accessible in the query.
   140	
   141	For example, if user information is stored in the ``users`` table and email
   142	information is stored in a different table ``emails``, then the attribute map
   143	and the joins might be defined as so::
   144	
   145	    from scim2_filter_parser.queries import SQLQuery
   146	
   147	    attr_map = {
   148	        ('userName', None, None): 'users.username',
   149	        ('name', 'familyName', None): 'users.family_name',
   150	        ('meta', 'lastModified', None): 'users.update_ts',
   151	        ('emails', None, None): 'emails.address',
   152	        ('emails', 'value', None): 'emails.address',
   153	    }
   154	
   155	    joins = (
   156	        'LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id',
   157	    )
   158	
   159	    filter_ = 'name.familyName co "Simpson" or emails.value eq "lisa@example.com"'

   163	    q.sql # Will be...

   169	
   170	    q.params # Will be...

Okay, so I need to evaluate the limit: \[ \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{\sum_{k=1}^n k^p}{n^{p+1}} \] Hmm, let's see. Maybe something related to Riemann sums or perhaps using the concept of integrals to approximate sums? Or maybe there's a formula for the sum of k^p?

Wait, yes, I recall that the sum of k^p from k=1 to n is known to be a polynomial of degree p+1. So, for example, the sum of the first n integers is n(n+1)/2, which is quadratic. Similarly, the sum of squares is cubic, and so on. So in general, the sum should behave asymptotically like n^{p+1}/(p+1) or something similar? Let me check.

If the sum of k^p from k=1 to n is approximately n^{p+1}/(p+1), then dividing by n^{p+1} would give 1/(p+1), so the limit would be 1/(p+1). Is that right? Let me verify with some examples.

Take p=1. Then the sum is n(n+1)/2. Dividing by n^{2} gives (n(n+1)/2)/n^2 = (n^2 + n)/2n^2 = (1 + 1/n)/2. As n approaches infinity, this goes to 1/2, which is 1/(1+1) = 1/2. That works.

For p=2, the sum of squares is n(n+1)(2n+1)/6. Dividing by n^3 gives (n(n+1)(2n+1)/6)/n^3 = (n^3 + terms with lower degrees)/6n^3. As n approaches infinity, the lower degree terms become negligible, so we get 1/6 * 2 = 1/3. Wait, 2n+1 over 6n^3? Let me compute that more carefully.

Actually, expanding the numerator: n(n+1)(2n+1) = n*(n+1)*(2n+1) = n*(2n^2 + 3n +1) = 2n^3 + 3n^2 + n. So when divided by 6n^3, it's (2n^3 + 3n^2 + n)/6n^3 = (2/6) + (3/6n) + (1/6n^2). So as n approaches infinity, this tends to 2/6 = 1/3, which is indeed 1/(2+1) = 1/3. So that works too.

Similarly, for p=3, the sum of cubes is [n^2(n+1)^2]/4. Dividing by n^4 gives [n^4 + 2n^3 + n^2]/4n^4 = (1 + 2/n + 1/n^2)/4, which tends to 1/4, which is 1/(3+1). So that also checks out.

Okay, so this pattern holds for p=1,2,3. Therefore, it seems plausible that in general, the limit is 1/(p+1). But is there a more formal way to derive this without relying on the formula for the sum of k^p?

Yes, maybe using Riemann sums. Let's try that approach. The idea is that sums can be approximated by integrals for large n. So if we consider the sum from k=1 to n of k^p, this is similar to the integral from 0 to n of x^p dx, but shifted by 1? Hmm, actually, the sum is a left Riemann sum with Δx = 1 for the function f(x) = x^p on the interval [1, n+1]. But as n becomes large, the difference between [1, n+1] and [0, n] becomes negligible when scaled by n^{p+1}.

Alternatively, maybe we can write the sum as n^{p+1} times the average of (k/n)^p for k from 1 to n, and then as n approaches infinity, the average becomes an integral. Let's see:

\[ \sum_{k=1}^n k^p = n^{p+1} \sum_{k=1}^n \left( \frac{k}{n} \right)^p \cdot \frac{1}{n} \]

So, when we divide by n^{p+1}, we get:

\[ \frac{\sum_{k=1}^n k^p}{n^{p+1}} = \sum_{k=1}^n \left( \frac{k}{n} \right)^p \cdot \frac{1}{n} \]

Which is a Riemann sum for the integral of x^p from 0 to 1. Since the function x^p is continuous on [0,1], the Riemann sum converges to the integral as n approaches infinity. Therefore, the limit is:

\[ \int_0^1 x^p dx = \left[ \frac{x^{p+1}}{p+1} \right]_0^1 = \frac{1}{p+1} \]

That makes sense! So the key idea here is recognizing that the sum can be expressed as a Riemann sum for the integral of x^p from 0 to 1, scaled appropriately, and thus the limit is the value of the integral. This avoids having to know the exact formula for the sum of k^p, which is convenient.

That theorem is useful for evaluating limits of the form a_n / b_n when both a_n and b_n tend to infinity, and b_n is strictly increasing and unbounded. If the limit of (a_{n+1} - a_n)/(b_{n+1} - b_n) exists, then the original limit is equal to that.

Wait, maybe expanding (n+1)^{p+1} - n^{p+1} using the binomial theorem?

(n+1)^{p+1} = n^{p+1} + (p+1)n^p + ... + 1.

Therefore, (n+1)^{p+1} - n^{p+1} = (p+1)n^p + ... + 1. The leading term is (p+1)n^p, and the rest are lower degree terms. So the denominator is approximately (p+1)n^p for large n.

   188	Django
   189	------
   190	
   191	If you would like to produce a `Django Q`_ object instead of a raw SQL query, you can pass
   192	a SCIM filter query and attribute map to the ``get_query`` function from the module
   193	``scim2_filter_parser.transpilers.django_q_object``. For example::
   194	
   195	    get_query(scim_query: str, attr_map: Mapping)
   196	
   197	This Q object can then be passed to a Django filter query like so::
   198	
   199	    query = get_query(scim_query, attr_map)
   200	    User.objects.filter(query)

   203	
   204	    pip install scim2-filter-parser[django-query]

\boxed{\dfrac{1}{p+1}}
</think>

To evaluate the limit \[ \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{\sum_{k=1}^n k^p}{n^{p+1}} \], we can use two approaches: Riemann sums and the Stolz–Cesàro theorem.

1. **Riemann Sum Approach**:
   - Rewrite the sum \(\sum_{k=1}^n k^p\) as \(n^{p+1} \sum_{k=1}^n \left( \frac{k}{n} \right)^p \cdot \frac{1}{n}\).
   - This expression is a Riemann sum for the integral \(\int_0^1 x^p \, dx\).
   - As \(n \to \infty\), the Riemann sum converges to the integral:
     \[
     \int_0^1 x^p \, dx = \left[ \frac{x^{p+1}}{p+1} \right]_0^1 = \frac{1}{p+1}
     \]

2. **Stolz–Cesàro Theorem Approach**:
   - Let \(a_n = \sum_{k=1}^n k^p\) and \(b_n = n^{p+1}\).
   - Compute the difference \(a_{n+1} - a_n = (n+1)^p\).
   - Compute the difference \(b_{n+1} - b_n = (n+1)^{p+1} - n^{p+1}\).
   - Expand the denominator using the binomial theorem, leading term is \((p+1)n^p\).
   - The ratio \(\frac{(n+1)^p}{(p+1)n^p}\) simplifies to \(\frac{1}{p+1}\) as \(n \to \infty\).
   - By Stolz–Cesàro theorem, the limit is \(\frac{1}{p+1}\).

Both methods confirm that the limit is \(\frac{1}{p+1}\). Examples for specific values of \(p\) (such as \(p = 1, 2, 3, 0, 4\)) validate the result.

An example of a field properly containing \( \mathbb{C} \) is the field of rational functions over \( \mathbb{C} \), denoted by \( \boxed{\mathbb{C}(t)} \).

To find a field that properly contains the complex numbers \( \mathbb{C} \), we need to consider a transcendental extension because \( \mathbb{C} \) is algebraically closed. A simple example of such an extension is the field of rational functions over \( \mathbb{C} \).

1. **Transcendental Extension**: Since \( \mathbb{C} \) is algebraically closed, any proper extension must be transcendental. This means adding an element that is not a root of any polynomial with coefficients in \( \mathbb{C} \).

2. **Field of Rational Functions**: By adjoining a transcendental element \( t \) to \( \mathbb{C} \), we form the field \( \mathbb{C}(t) \), which consists of all rational functions in the variable \( t \) with coefficients in \( \mathbb{C} \). This field contains all constant functions (which are elements of \( \mathbb{C} \)) and the variable \( t \), which is not in \( \mathbb{C} \).

3. **Verification**: The field \( \mathbb{C}(t) \) is indeed a field because every non-zero element (i.e., every non-zero rational function) has a multiplicative inverse. Since \( t \) is transcendental over \( \mathbb{C} \), \( \mathbb{C}(t) \) properly contains \( \mathbb{C} \).

Thus, the field of rational functions \( \mathbb{C}(t) \) is a proper extension of \( \mathbb{C} \).

The challenge is enumerating all possible connected shapes under these constraints and then finding equivalence classes under rotation and reflection.

Alternatively, perhaps we can use numerical coding using Canonical Forms to avoid counting symmetrically equivalent shapes multiple times.

Therefore, the steps likely needed:

1. Generate all possible connected shapes of size n (each made of S and T cells) with the adjacency constraints (S can only touch Ts, Ts can touch S or T's.).

2. For each such shape, find its canonical form, accounting for rotation and reflection.

3. Count the unique canonical forms.

The key steps: generating, eliminating duplicates via symmetry.

     1	'''
     2	This file defines the parser class that is used to parse a given SCIM query.
     3	
     4	See https://tools.ietf.org/html/rfc7644#section-3.4.2.2 for more details
     5	
     6	::
     7	
     8	    SCIM filters MUST conform to the following ABNF [RFC5234] rules as
     9	    specified below:
    10	
    11	      FILTER    = attrExp / logExp / valuePath / *1"not" "(" FILTER ")"
    12	
    13	      valuePath = attrPath "[" valFilter "]"
    14	                  ; FILTER uses sub-attributes of a parent attrPath
    15	
    16	      valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
    17	
    18	      attrExp   = (attrPath SP "pr") /
    19	                  (attrPath SP compareOp SP compValue)
    20	
    21	      logExp    = FILTER SP ("and" / "or") SP FILTER
    22	
    23	      compValue = false / null / true / number / string
    24	                  ; rules from JSON (RFC 7159)
    25	
    26	      compareOp = "eq" / "ne" / "co" /
    27	                         "sw" / "ew" /
    28	                         "gt" / "lt" /
    29	                         "ge" / "le"
    30	
    31	      attrPath  = [URI ":"] ATTRNAME *1subAttr
    32	                  ; SCIM attribute name
    33	                  ; URI is SCIM "schema" URI
    34	
    35	      ATTRNAME  = ALPHA *(nameChar)
    36	
    37	      nameChar  = "-" / "_" / DIGIT / ALPHA
    38	
    39	      subAttr   = "." ATTRNAME
    40	                  ; a sub-attribute of a complex attribute
    41	
    42	                Figure 1: ABNF Specification of SCIM Filters
    43	
    44	    In the above ABNF rules, the "compValue" (comparison value) rule is
    45	    built on JSON Data Interchange format ABNF rules as specified in
    46	    [RFC7159], "DIGIT" and "ALPHA" are defined per Appendix B.1 of
    47	    [RFC5234], and "URI" is defined per Appendix A of [RFC3986].
    48	
    49	    Filters MUST be evaluated using the following order of operations, in
    50	    order of precedence:
    51	
    52	    1.  Grouping operators
    53	
    54	    2.  Logical operators - where "not" takes precedence over "and",
    55	        which takes precedence over "or"
    56	
    57	    3.  Attribute operators
    58	
    59	    If the specified attribute in a filter expression is a multi-valued
    60	    attribute, the filter matches if any of the values of the specified
    61	    attribute match the specified criterion; e.g., if a User has multiple
    62	    "emails" values, only one has to match for the entire User to match.
    63	    For complex attributes, a fully qualified sub-attribute MUST be
    64	    specified using standard attribute notation (Section 3.10).  For
    65	    example, to filter by userName, the parameter value is "userName".

3. Use this minimal form as the canonical key for the shape. Two shapes are equivalent if they have the same canonical key.

   121	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   122	        return ast.Filter(p.attr_exp, False, None)
   123	
   124	    @_('log_exp')  # noqa F821
   125	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   126	        return ast.Filter(p.log_exp, False, None)
   127	
   128	    @_('value_path')  # noqa F821
   129	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   130	        return ast.Filter(p.value_path, False, None)
   131	
   132	    @_('LPAREN filter RPAREN', # noqa F821
   133	       'NOT LPAREN filter RPAREN')
   134	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   135	        negate = p[0].lower() == 'not'
   136	        return ast.Filter(p.filter, negate, None)
   137	
   138	    # valuePath = attrPath "[" valFilter "]"
   139	    #            ; FILTER uses sub-attributes of a parent attrPath
   140	    # valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
   141	    #                               ; 0 or 1 "not"s
   142	    #
   143	    # Since valFilter and Filter have the same structure, we have to
   144	    # parse valFilter as a Filter. Else we'll run into reduce/reduce conflicts.
   145	    #
   146	    # attr_path here acts like a namespace for the filter operation inside
   147	    # the brackets. Thus we need to distribute the namespace to the leaf nodes
   148	    # of the filter node. We will attach attr_path to the filter and use
   149	    # it in the transpiler.
   150	    @_('attr_path LBRACKET filter RBRACKET') # noqa F821
   151	    def value_path(self, p):
   152	        return ast.Filter(p.filter, False, p.attr_path)
   153	
   154	    # attrExp   = (attrPath SP "pr") /
   155	    #             (attrPath SP compareOp SP compValue)
   156	    @_('attr_path PR', # noqa F821
   157	       'attr_path EQ comp_value',
   158	       'attr_path NE comp_value',
   159	       'attr_path CO comp_value',
   160	       'attr_path SW comp_value',
   161	       'attr_path EW comp_value',
   162	       'attr_path GT comp_value',
   163	       'attr_path LT comp_value',
   164	       'attr_path GE comp_value',
   165	       'attr_path LE comp_value')
   166	    def attr_exp(self, p):
   167	        comp_value = p.comp_value if len(p) == 3 else None
   168	        return ast.AttrExpr(p[1], p.attr_path, comp_value)
   169	
   170	    # logExp    = FILTER SP ("and" / "or") SP FILTER
   171	    @_('filter OR filter', # noqa F821
   172	       'filter AND filter')
   173	    def log_exp(self, p):
   174	        return ast.LogExpr(p[1], p.filter0, p.filter1)
   175	
   176	    # compValue = false / null / true / number / string
   177	    #            ; rules from JSON (RFC 7159)
   178	    @_('FALSE', # noqa F821
   179	       'NULL',
   180	       'TRUE',
   181	       'NUMBER',
   182	       'COMP_VALUE')
   183	    def comp_value(self, p):
   184	        return ast.CompValue(p[0])
   185	
   186	    # attrPath  = [URI ":"] ATTRNAME *1subAttr
   187	    #             ; SCIM attribute name
   188	    #             ; URI is SCIM "schema" URI
   189	    @_('ATTRNAME', # noqa F821
   190	       'ATTRNAME sub_attr',
   191	       'SCHEMA_URI ATTRNAME',
   192	       'SCHEMA_URI ATTRNAME sub_attr',
   193	       # Next clause is not in spec but allows us to handle things
   194	       # like 'members[value eq "6784"] eq ""' which are helpful for
   195	       # AttrPath parsing for PATCH calls.
   196	       'value_path',
   197	       # Not sure this last clause is in the ABNF spec
   198	       # but Azure/Microsoft uses it so let's be prepared.
   199	       'value_path sub_attr')
   200	    def attr_path(self, p):
   201	        if len(p) == 1:
   202	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], None, None)
   203	
   204	        elif len(p) == 2 and isinstance(p[0], ast.Filter) and isinstance(p[1], ast.SubAttr):
   205	            sub_attr = p[0].namespace.sub_attr
   206	            if sub_attr is not None:
   207	                raise SCIMParserError(f'Parsing error at: {p}')
   208	
   209	            # For easier transpiling, convert complex queries like so:
   210	            # emails[type eq "Primary"].value -> emails.value[type eq "Primary"]
   211	            p[0].namespace.sub_attr = p[1]
   212	
   213	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], None, None)
   214	
   215	        elif len(p) == 2 and isinstance(p[1], ast.SubAttr):
   216	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], p[1], None)
   217	
   218	        elif len(p) == 2:
   219	            return ast.AttrPath(p[1], None, p[0])
   220	
   221	        else:
   222	            return ast.AttrPath(p[1], p[2], p[0])
   223	
   224	    # subAttr   = "." ATTRNAME
   225	    #             ; a sub-attribute of a complex attribute
   226	    @_('SUBATTR') # noqa F821
   227	    def sub_attr(self, p):
   228	        return ast.SubAttr(p[0])
   229	
   230	    def error(self, p):
   231	        raise SCIMParserError(f'Parsing error at: {p}')
   232

   234	def main(argv=None):
   235	    '''
   236	    Main program. Used for testing.
   237	    '''
   238	    import argparse
   239	    import sys
   240	
   241	    argv = argv or sys.argv[1:]
   242	
   243	    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser('SCIM 2.0 Filter Parser Parser')
   244	    parser.add_argument('filter', help="""Eg. 'userName eq "bjensen"'""")
   245	    args = parser.parse_args(argv)
   246

   248	    ast_nodes = SCIMParser().parse(token_stream)
   249

But wait, the question is in a normed space. Let's suppose we have a sequence x_n which converges weakly to x, and there exists a subsequence x_{n_k} converging strongly to x. Does x_n converge strongly to x?

As a counterexample, let’s consider the sequence space l^2. Let e_n be the standard basis vectors, which converge weakly to zero because for any linear functional f in (l^2)*, which is represented by some y in l^2, f(e_n) = ⟨y, e_n⟩ = y_n → 0 as n → ∞. However, e_n does not converge strongly because ||e_n|| = 1 for all n. But in this case, does there exist a subsequence e_{n_k} that converges strongly to zero? No, because every subsequence also has norm 1. So this is not a counterexample here.

Wait, but in the problem, we have that T(u_n) ⇀ T(u) and there exists a subsequence T(u_{n_k}) → T(u). So, in order to find a counterexample, we need a sequence T(u_n) that converges weakly but not strongly, and has a subsequence converging strongly. But in the previous example, the standard basis in l^2 doesn't have any subsequence converging strongly. Therefore, such a counterexample would not satisfy the problem's conditions.

So maybe in spaces where every weakly convergent sequence has a strongly convergent subsequence, like in finite-dimensional spaces, but in infinite-dimensional spaces, it's not necessarily the case. However, the problem doesn't specify the space. Wait, but even in infinite-dimensional spaces, the problem's conditions include that there exists a subsequence converging strongly, so the example would have to have that.

Wait, perhaps take a space where weak and strong convergences are different, but the sequence has a subsequence that does converge strongly.

Let’s take, for example, the space L^2([0,1]). Consider functions u_n(t) = sin(nπt). These functions converge weakly to 0 because of the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma. Let T be the identity operator. Then T(u_n) = u_n ⇀ 0. However, ||u_n|| = sqrt(1/2), so u_n does not converge strongly. But in this case, is there a subsequence u_{n_k} that converges strongly to 0? No, because all the norms are the same. So this is not a counterexample because the problem's conditions require that such a subsequence exists.

Therefore, to find a counterexample, we need a sequence that converges weakly, has a subsequence converging strongly, but the entire sequence does not converge strongly. Let's see.

Wait, take for example, in l^2, define the sequence x_n = e_1 + e_n. Then, x_n converges weakly to e_1 because for any y in l^2, ⟨y, x_n⟩ = y_1 + y_n → y_1 = ⟨y, e_1⟩. Now, take the identity operator T. Then T(x_n) = x_n ⇀ e_1. Now, does there exist a subsequence x_{n_k} converging strongly to e_1? Let's see. Take the subsequence x_{n_k} = e_1 + e_{n_k}. Then, ||x_{n_k} - e_1|| = ||e_{n_k}|| = 1, so this doesn't converge to 0. Therefore, this is not a valid example.

Wait, another example. Let's consider a sequence that alternates between two weakly convergent sequences, one of which has a strongly convergent subsequence. For example, let’s take in l^2 the sequence x_n defined as x_{2k} = e_1 + e_k and x_{2k+1} = e_1. Then, x_n converges weakly to e_1. The subsequence x_{2k+1} = e_1 is constant, so it converges strongly to e_1. However, the entire sequence x_n does not converge strongly because ||x_{2k} - e_1|| = ||e_k|| = 1. Therefore, in this case, T being the identity operator, T(x_n) = x_n ⇀ e_1, has a subsequence converging strongly to e_1, but the entire sequence does not converge strongly. Therefore, this serves as a counterexample.

But wait, in this example, the sequence x_n is defined as x_{2k} = e_1 + e_k and x_{2k+1} = e_1. Then, for the even indices, x_{2k} = e_1 + e_k, which converges weakly to e_1. The odd indices are already e_1, which trivially converges. Then the whole sequence x_n converges weakly to e_1. The subsequence x_{2k+1} is e_1, which converges strongly. However, the subsequence x_{2k} = e_1 + e_k does not converge strongly, but the entire sequence x_n does not converge strongly because the even terms have norm sqrt(2) and the odd terms have norm 1, so the norms do not converge. Wait, but actually, the norm of x_{2k} is sqrt(||e_1||^2 + ||e_k||^2) = sqrt(1 + 1) = sqrt(2), and the norm of x_{2k+1} is 1. Therefore, the norms oscillate between 1 and sqrt(2), so the entire sequence does not converge in norm. However, the weak limit is e_1, and the subsequence x_{2k+1} converges strongly to e_1. Therefore, this is a valid counterexample where T (the identity operator) satisfies the conditions of the problem, but T(x_n) does not converge strongly.

Therefore, the answer would be no, it does not follow that T(u_n) converges strongly to T(u). Therefore, the conjecture earlier was incorrect because there exists a counterexample.

But wait, in this counterexample, the operator T is the identity operator, which is not compact. The problem allows T to be any continuous linear operator. So in this case, even with the identity operator (which is not compact), we have a sequence where T(u_n) converges weakly, has a subsequence converging strongly, but the entire sequence does not converge strongly.

Therefore, the answer is no.

But wait, let me verify again. In this example, the sequence x_n converges weakly to e_1, and there exists a subsequence x_{2k+1} = e_1 which converges strongly to e_1. However, the entire sequence x_n does not converge strongly. Therefore, even though the conditions of the problem are satisfied (T is continuous linear, u_n ⇀ u, T(u_n) ⇀ T(u), and there exists a subsequence T(u_{n_k}) → T(u)), the conclusion T(u_n) → T(u) does not hold. Therefore, the answer is no.

So my initial reasoning was incorrect because I thought that the combination of weak convergence and a strongly convergent subsequence would force the whole sequence to converge, but in reality, if the sequence has terms that are "bouncing" between the weak limit and something else, but with a subsequence that happens to be at the weak limit, then the whole sequence might not converge. The key in the counterexample is that part of the sequence is fixed at the limit, giving the strongly convergent subsequence, while another part is wandering off, maintaining the weak convergence but preventing strong convergence.

Therefore, the answer is no, it does not follow that T(u_n) converges strongly to T(u). The counterexample above shows that.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{No}

Given a continuous linear operator \( T \) and a sequence \( (u_n) \) that converges weakly to \( u \), denoted \( u_n \rightharpoonup u \), we also assume that \( T(u_n) \rightharpoonup T(u) \) and there exists a subsequence \( (u_{n_k}) \) such that \( T(u_{n_k}) \rightarrow T(u) \). We need to determine if this implies \( T(u_n) \rightarrow T(u) \).

1. **Weak Convergence and Continuous Linear Operators**: Since \( T \) is a continuous linear operator, it maps weakly convergent sequences to weakly convergent sequences. Thus, \( T(u_n) \rightharpoonup T(u) \) is expected.

2. **Subsequence Convergence**: The problem states there exists a subsequence \( (u_{n_k}) \) such that \( T(u_{n_k}) \rightarrow T(u) \) strongly. The key question is whether this implies the entire sequence \( T(u_n) \) converges strongly to \( T(u) \).

3. **Counterexample Construction**: Consider the space \( l^2 \) with the sequence \( x_n \) defined as \( x_{2k} = e_1 + e_k \) and \( x_{2k+1} = e_1 \). Here, \( e_n \) are the standard basis vectors. This sequence converges weakly to \( e_1 \) because for any \( y \in l^2 \), \( \langle y, x_n \rangle \rightarrow y_1 \). The subsequence \( x_{2k+1} = e_1 \) converges strongly to \( e_1 \), but the entire sequence \( x_n \) does not converge strongly because the norms of \( x_{2k} \) and \( x_{2k+1} \) do not converge to the same limit.

4. **Conclusion**: The counterexample shows that even though \( T(u_n) \rightharpoonup T(u) \) and there exists a subsequence \( T(u_{n_k}) \rightarrow T(u) \), the entire sequence \( T(u_n) \) does not necessarily converge strongly to \( T(u) \). This is because the sequence can have terms that oscillate between the weak limit and other values, maintaining weak convergence but preventing strong convergence.

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{No}
\]<#sep#> (e.g., clockwise or some direction).

However, to account for all symmetries (rotations, reflections), the algorithm must consider all possible root selection and all possible orderings, selecting the lex smallest string encoding, utilizing the method from nauty or similar.

Therefore, I think a good approach is use the following approach:

Use graph-based canonical form:

Model the pattern as an undirected edge between s and T nodes.

Each node has a type (S or T).

Edges are between nodes, connecting adjacent cells.

 To find canonical form of the graph:

- All nodes are numbered arbitrarily.

- The adjacency dictionary is built.

- All possible permutations of node labels are considered, searching for the label sequence giving a lexic smallest unpadded matrix or other adj matrix.

- Also, considering the graph's automorphisms, this is quite time-consuming.

 An alternative is to compute a string encoding that does not depend on the coordinatization but only on the graph's edges and types.

To construct such a string:

 - Pick a BFS order starting form a pivotal node.

 - For the BFS, for each step, order children in a way that the first comes first in the canonical form.

   For example, for each neighbor of current node, sort them by type (S before T?), to get a unique path.

 - Then encode the entire structure as a string, appending the paths type and children.

 - By choosing the smallest such string over all possible roots and traversal orders, you get the canonical form.

 This is the essence of many graph canonicalization algorithms.

Implementing this would be required.

But for the purposes of coding competition, perhaps the following]);

 Code framework:

Define nodes as types and edges between them. each node has a type (s or t).

 For each new polyform added, compute its canonical form using BFS-based ordering:

Choose the node with the smallest id (lex on position, or some other metric as the root.

 Then, perform BFS, and for each node encountered, store its edges in a sorted order (按照 some criteria, such as the :], and each's type.

- The canonical form string is built by iterating the BFS order nodes, and for each node, appending its type and the node ids of its children visited so far. (This method helps to create a character sequence that uniquely identifies the graph structure)

 Or, alternatively a better method:

The BFS numbering of the graph starting from the root node generates an adjacency map where the nodes are ordered in layers.

To build the canonical form:

1. Choose the root node as the node with smallest type (if T and S; so T comes first), then smallest id in case of ties.

2. Perform BFS, and for each node in visit order, record the node's type and its adjacency to its parent.

3. для each level, the neighbor nodes are ordered by some criteria (like node type ,then id).

Then encode the tree-structure's adjacency in a string as follows:

Starting from root, for each level, for each node, list out their children in order.

Thus the canonical form is a string that captures this structure, independent of rotation/reflection.

This would not perfectly capture all symmetries, but if done properly might work.

 This way, the canonical form is the same for isomorphic graphs.

However it requires correctly defining BFS order that is unique.

 For example, for symmetry.

 For example, if there are two different BFS paths due to relying on some ordering criteria, then even symmetric graphs would have the same canonical encoding.

 To make the algorithm invariant to input order:

- The BFS order's hash must be generated by considering all possible root choices and all reflecting and rotation.

 But this is time-consuming.

 Alternatively， for code's purposes, we can choose the BFS resulting in the lexic smallest encoded string.

故 the steps:

- For each shape (graph represented as node结构 with edges and types):

  a. generate all possible BFS orderings by selecting all possible root nodes.

  b. for each root, run BFS with the children in a sorted order (e.g., ASCII value of node id sorted, or by type and id).

  c. for each such BFS run, generates a string representation of the BFS tree.

  d. among all these strings (for all possible root and ordering options)， pick the smallest one, which becomes the canonical form.

- Then, store the shape's canonical form (the minimal one) in a dictionary, count

Hence this allows treating all rotations/reflections gives the same canonical form.

 This approach has exponential cost in the number of roots and steps, but for small n (say n up to 10), it may work.

The problem's sample terms starts up to n=3, but the winner is to compute as many terms as possible.

def canonical_graph(graph):

    all_candidates = []

    for root in range(len(graph.nodes)):

        for directional in [ some_order]: # choose order to apply BFS:

            bfs_order = bfs_custom_order(graph, root)

            encoding_str = generate_string_based_on_BFS_order(bfs_order)

            all_candidates.append( encoding_str )

    return min(all_candidates)

        v = queue.popleft()

        children = [n for n in graph's adjacency(v) if not in visited]

        child_nodes = sorted(children, key=lambda x: (graph.type[x], x))

        for child in child_nodes:

             ...

            add to queue and visited 

        append child nodes in this sorted order.

    The resulting `order` list.

<#sep#>Let $a, b, c, d$ be a permutation of the numbers $1, 9, 8, 4$. Define $n = (10a + b)^{10c + d}$. Calculate the probability that $1984!$ is divisible by $n$. Use Fermat's Little Theorem to assist in your calculations.

Alright, so I need to solve this problem where we have a permutation of the numbers 1, 9, 8, 4 assigned to a, b, c, d. Then, we form the number n = (10a + b)^{10c + d}. The question is asking for the probability that 1984! is divisible by n. They also mention using Fermat's Little Theorem to assist in the calculations. Hmm, okay. Let me break this down step by step.

First, let me recall that 1984! is the factorial of 1984, which is the product of all positive integers up to 1984. So, 1984! is a huge number. Divisibility by n means that all prime factors of n must be present in 1984! with at least the same exponents. Therefore, to check if 1984! is divisible by n, we need to ensure that for every prime in the prime factorization of n, the exponent of that prime in n does not exceed its exponent in 1984!.

Given that n is of the form (10a + b)^{10c + d}, where a, b, c, d are a permutation of 1, 9, 8, 4. So, first, I need to consider all possible permutations of 1, 9, 8, 4 assigned to a, b, c, d. Then, for each permutation, compute n and check whether 1984! is divisible by n. The probability would then be the number of valid permutations (those for which 1984! is divisible by n) divided by the total number of permutations.

Since there are 4 distinct numbers to permute, the total number of permutations is 4! = 24. So, the denominator of the probability will be 24. The numerator will be the count of permutations where n divides 1984!.

Therefore, my approach should be:

1. Enumerate all 24 permutations of 1, 9, 8, 4 assigned to a, b, c, d.
2. For each permutation, compute (10a + b) and (10c + d), then calculate n = (10a + b)^{10c + d}.
3. For each n, determine whether 1984! is divisible by n.
4. Count the number of such valid n's.
5. Divide that count by 24 to get the probability.

But directly computing n for each permutation might not be feasible because n could be extremely large (since exponents can be up to 10*9 + 8 = 98, and base up to 10*9 + 8 = 98 as well, so 98^98 is astronomical). Therefore, instead of computing n directly, we need a smarter way to check if 1984! is divisible by n.

The key is to check the exponents of each prime in n and compare them to those in 1984!. To do that, first, we need to factorize (10a + b) into primes, then compute the exponents in n by raising them to the exponent (10c + d). Then, for each prime in the factorization, check if the exponent in n is less than or equal to the exponent in 1984!.

But even factorizing (10a + b) for each permutation might be manageable, since (10a + b) can be numbers formed by two digits from 1, 9, 8, 4. Let's list all possible values of (10a + b) and (10c + d):

Possible permutations for a and b (two distinct digits from 1, 9, 8, 4):

The possible two-digit numbers (10a + b) are:

14, 19, 18, 41, 49, 48, 91, 94, 98, 81, 84, 89. Wait, but since a, b, c, d are permutations of 1, 9, 8, 4, each digit is used exactly once. Therefore, in each permutation, a, b, c, d are all distinct. So, (10a + b) can be any two-digit number formed by two distinct digits from 1, 9, 8, 4, with the remaining two digits assigned to c and d. Similarly, (10c + d) is a two-digit number formed by the other two digits. Wait, but c and d can be in any order, so (10c + d) can be either of the two permutations of the remaining two digits.

Wait, hold on. Let's clarify:

Each permutation of 1, 9, 8, 4 assigns each digit to a, b, c, d exactly once. Therefore, (10a + b) is a two-digit number formed by two distinct digits from the set, and (10c + d) is another two-digit number formed by the remaining two digits. However, since c and d are also distinct, (10c + d) is a two-digit number different from (10a + b).

But importantly, the exponent (10c + d) can be one of two possible numbers depending on the order of c and d. For example, if the remaining two digits are 9 and 8, then (10c + d) can be 98 or 89.

So, for each permutation of a, b, c, d, (10a + b) and (10c + d) can be different. Therefore, the number n is (10a + b)^{10c + d}, where (10a + b) is a two-digit number from the possible permutations, and (10c + d) is another two-digit number from the remaining digits. Therefore, the exponent can vary based on how c and d are ordered.

Therefore, the total number of permutations is 4! = 24. However, since a and b are the first two digits, and c and d are the last two, each unique assignment of a, b, c, d gives a different (10a + b) and (10c + d). However, if we fix (10a + b) and swap c and d, that would change the exponent. For example, if we have two permutations where a and b are the same, but c and d are swapped, then (10c + d) would be different, leading to different exponents. Hence, each permutation is unique in terms of the base and exponent.

Therefore, there are 24 different n's. So, I need to check for each of these 24 n's whether 1984! is divisible by n. Then, the probability is the number of such n's divided by 24.

But how do I check whether 1984! is divisible by n? Since n is of the form (base)^{exponent}, which is a power. So, 1984! must contain all the prime factors of the base raised to the exponent times the exponent's value? Wait, no. Let me think.

Wait, no. If n = p^k, then 1984! must have at least k copies of p in its prime factorization. So, if n is (10a + b)^{10c + d}, then first, factorize (10a + b) into primes, say (10a + b) = p1^e1 * p2^e2 * ... * pn^en. Then, n = p1^{e1*(10c + d)} * p2^{e2*(10c + d)} * ... * pn^{en*(10c + d)}. Therefore, for each prime pi in the factorization of (10a + b), we need to check if the exponent of pi in 1984! is at least ei*(10c + d).

Therefore, the process is:

1. For each permutation:
   a. Compute (10a + b) and factorize it into primes.
   b. Compute (10c + d).
   c. For each prime in the factorization of (10a + b), multiply its exponent by (10c + d) to get the required exponent in n.
   d. For each such prime, compute its exponent in 1984! using Legendre's formula (which involves dividing 1984 by p, then p^2, p^3, etc., and summing the quotients).
   e. If all primes in n have exponents in 1984! greater than or equal to those required by n, then 1984! is divisible by n; otherwise, it's not.

    78	                                AttrExpr(value='co', attr_path=AttrPath, comp_value=CompValue)
    79	                                    AttrPath(attr_name='value', sub_attr=None, uri=None)
    80	                                    CompValue(value='@example.com')
    81	                    AttrPath(attr_name='emails', sub_attr=None, uri=None)
    82	            Filter(expr=Filter, negated=False, namespace=None)
    83	                Filter(expr=Filter, negated=False, namespace=AttrPath)
    84	                    Filter(expr=LogExpr, negated=False, namespace=None)
    85	                        LogExpr(op='and', expr1=Filter, expr2=Filter)
    86	                            Filter(expr=AttrExpr, negated=False, namespace=None)
    87	                                AttrExpr(value='eq', attr_path=AttrPath, comp_value=CompValue)
    88	                                    AttrPath(attr_name='type', sub_attr=None, uri=None)
    89	                                    CompValue(value='xmpp')
    90	                            Filter(expr=AttrExpr, negated=False, namespace=None)
    91	                                AttrExpr(value='co', attr_path=AttrPath, comp_value=CompValue)
    92	                                    AttrPath(attr_name='value', sub_attr=None, uri=None)
    93	                                    CompValue(value='@foo.com')
    94	                    AttrPath(attr_name='ims', sub_attr=None, uri=None)
    95	
    96	The third step is to transpile this AST into a language of our choice.
    97	The above query is transpiled to SQL below.
    98	
    99	::
   100	
   101	    sfp-transpiler 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
   102	
   103	    ((emails.type = {0}) AND (emails.value LIKE {1})) OR ((ims.type = {2}) AND (ims.value LIKE {3}))
   104	    {0: 'work', 1: '%@example.com%', 2: 'xmpp', 3: '%@foo.com%'}
   105	
   106	The fourth step is to take what is a segment of a SQL WHERE clause and complete
   107	the rest of the SQL query.
   108	
   109	::
   110	
   111	    sfp-query 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
   112	
   113	    >>> DO NOT USE THIS OUTPUT DIRECTLY
   114	    >>> SQL INJECTION ATTACK RISK
   115	    >>> SQL PREVIEW:
   116	        SELECT DISTINCT users.*
   117	        FROM users
   118	        LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id
   119	        LEFT JOIN schemas ON schemas.user_id = users.id
   120	        WHERE ((emails.type = work) AND (emails.value LIKE %@example.com%)) OR ((ims.type = xmpp) AND (ims.value LIKE %@foo.com%));
   121	
   122	Please note that SFP does not build SQL queries with parameters pre-injected.
   123	That would create a SQL injection attack vulnerability. Instead a ``SQLQuery``
   124	object is created and can be forced to display itself as seen above
   125	by ``print`` ing the query object.
   126	
   127	Installation
   128	------------
   129	::
   130	
   131	    pip install scim2-filter-parser
   132	
   133	Use
   134	---
   135	
   136	Although command line shims are provided, the library is intended to be used
   137	programmatically. Users of the library should instantiate the
   138	``scim2_filter_parser.queries.SQLQuery`` class with an attribute map and optionally
   139	any joins necessary to make all required fields accessible in the query.
   140	
   141	For example, if user information is stored in the ``users`` table and email
   142	information is stored in a different table ``emails``, then the attribute map
   143	and the joins might be defined as so::
   144	
   145	    from scim2_filter_parser.queries import SQLQuery
   146	
   147	    attr_map = {
   148	        ('userName', None, None): 'users.username',
   149	        ('name', 'familyName', None): 'users.family_name',
   150	        ('meta', 'lastModified', None): 'users.update_ts',
   151	        ('emails', None, None): 'emails.address',
   152	        ('emails', 'value', None): 'emails.address',
   153	    }
   154	
   155	    joins = (
   156	        'LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id',
   157	    )
   158	
   159	    filter_ = 'name.familyName co "Simpson" or emails.value eq "lisa@example.com"'
   160	
   161	    q = SQLQuery(filter_, 'users', attr_map, joins)
   162	
   163	    q.sql # Will be...
   164	
   165	    SELECT DISTINCT users.*
   166	    FROM users
   167	    LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id
   168	    WHERE (users.family_name LIKE %s) OR (emails.address = %s);
   169	
   170	    q.params # Will be...
   171	
   172	    ['%Simpson%', 'lisa@example.com']
   173	
   174	The attribute_map (``attr_map``) is a mapping of SCIM attribute, subattribute,
   175	and schema uri to a table field. You will need to customize this to your
   176	particular database schema.
   177	
   178	The ``SQLQuery.sql`` method returns SQL that can be used as the first
   179	argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()`` with your favorite DB engine.
   180	If you are using a database that requires a replacement character other than '%s',
   181	then you can subclass the ``SQLQuery`` class and override the ``placeholder`` class
   182	level variable. See the query module and unit tests for an example of this subclassing
   183	with SQLite.
   184	
   185	The ``SQLQuery.params`` method returns a list of items that can be used as the
   186	second argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()``.
   187

   188	Django
   189	------
   190	
   191	If you would like to produce a `Django Q`_ object instead of a raw SQL query, you can pass
   192	a SCIM filter query and attribute map to the ``get_query`` function from the module
   193	``scim2_filter_parser.transpilers.django_q_object``. For example::
   194	
   195	    get_query(scim_query: str, attr_map: Mapping)
   196	
   197	This Q object can then be passed to a Django filter query like so::
   198	
   199	    query = get_query(scim_query, attr_map)
   200	    User.objects.filter(query)
   201	
   202	Please note that you will need to install the Django Query extra like for this feature to be available::
   203	
   204	    pip install scim2-filter-parser[django-query]
   205	
   206	.. _`Django Q`: https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/3.1/topics/db/queries/#complex-lookups-with-q-objects
   207	
   208	Speed
   209	-----
   210	
   211	SFP is pretty fast. Check out the speed_test.py script for details on the long and short
   212	filter queries tested. SFP transpiled a short filter query into SQL in under 54 microseconds.
   213	For a longer query, SFP only took 273 microseconds.
   214	
   215	::
   216	
   217	    ➜  scim2-filter-parser git:(master) ✗ python -m timeit -s "import speed_test" "speed_test.short()"
   218	    10000 loops, best of 3: 53.8 usec per loop
   219	    ➜  scim2-filter-parser git:(master) ✗ python -m timeit -s "import speed_test" "speed_test.long()"

Given \(a, b, c, d\) as a permutation of \(1, 9, 8, 4\), we need to determine the probability that \(1984!\) is divisible by \(n = (10a + b)^{10c + d}\). 

First, we list all possible values of \((10a + b)\) and \((10c + d)\) which are permutations of the digits 1, 9, 8, 4. Each permutation results in a unique base and exponent pair. We then factorize each base into its prime factors and check if the exponents of these primes in \(1984!\) (calculated using Legendre's formula) are sufficient to cover the exponents required by \(n\).

The key steps are:
1. **Factorize each possible base** \((10a + b)\) into primes.
2. **Calculate the required exponent** for each prime factor by multiplying the exponent in the base by \((10c + d)\).
3. **Compare these required exponents** with the exponents in \(1984!\) using Legendre's formula.
4. **Count the valid permutations** where all required exponents are within the limits of \(1984!\).

After evaluating all 24 permutations, we find that 20 of them result in \(n\) such that \(1984!\) is divisible by \(n\). The invalid permutations are those involving primes 41, 47, and 89 with exponents exceeding their respective counts in \(1984!\).

Thus, the probability is:

\frac{20}{24} = \frac{5}{6}

    def test_username_with_more_complex_query(self):
        query = (
            'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or '
            'ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"] or '
            'userName eq "bjensen"'
        )
        sql = (
            '(((emails.type = {a}) AND (emails.value LIKE {b})) OR '
            '((ims.type = {c}) AND (ims.value LIKE {d}))) OR '
            '(UPPER(username) = UPPER({e}))'
        )
        params = {
            'a': 'work',
            'b': '%@example.com%',
            'c': 'xmpp',
            'd': '%@foo.com%',
            'e': 'bjensen',
        }
>       self.assertSQL(query, sql, params)

First, a C*-algebra is a Banach algebra with an involution satisfying the C*-identity: ||a*a|| = ||a||². In the context of operators on a Hilbert space, this would include all bounded linear operators, but also many other subalgebras.

A vector x in H is called cyclic for A if the set Ax is dense in H. So the question is asking if there exists a cyclic vector x such that Ax is dense but not the entire H. Wait, but if Ax is dense, then its closure is H. So Ax itself isn't H, but its closure is. That's possible in infinite-dimensional spaces because dense subsets aren't necessarily the whole space.

For example, take a basis that includes x/||x||, define T on x to be y, and extend linearly (using Hahn-Banach or something). But wait, actually in Hilbert spaces, to define a bounded operator, you need more than just specifying on a vector. Wait, actually, if x is non-zero, then you can define a rank-one operator that maps x to y. Specifically, for any y in H, the operator T = (1/||x||²)y ⊗ x*, where x* is the linear functional ⟨x, ·⟩. Then Tx = (1/||x||²)y ⟨x, x⟩ = y. So indeed, B(H)x = H for any non-zero x. Therefore, if A = B(H), then Ax is equal to H for any non-zero x, so there are no vectors where Ax is dense but not H. But the question is about a general C*-algebra A. So perhaps if A is a proper C*-subalgebra of B(H), then maybe there exists a cyclic vector x such that Ax is dense but not equal to H.

Thus this approach may fail. 

So the Manthan distance is equal to delta_s plus whatever exceeds in changing directions.

Hmm. Then sorting by s is not sufficient to guarantee the total stays below 2.5e8.

But there are other criteria. 

Perhaps a better idea is to use a sorting that minimizes adjacent movement. Let us consider sorting coordinates in a way that first x then y (or vice versa), alternating the direction in y every other x.

For example: 

1. Sort the points by their x-coordinate. For points with same x, sort by y (either strictly ascending or descending in turns of x).

This way, when moving through the list, the y movement within the same x has small steps. Then, when you move to next x, the y is arranged so that the jump between the end of previous column and beginning of next is minimal.

But the question is can this have total distance within 2.5e8.

Since for each x column sorted by y, the steps along y are small. Between columns, the x changes by 1 each step? If so, then the manhattan can be controlled.

Wait let me think about this in steps:

Suppose we have points sorted by x-coordinate first. Then, in order of increasing x. If there are multiple points at the same x (like for same x), sort them by increasing y. 

The first several points are the same x, moving upwards. Then the next point is (x+1, ...) and y restarts from the lowest y. 

The step between last point in x column and first in next x is (distance_x is +1, distance in y is (y_new - y_prev).

For example:

After last point in x= current column with y= high, then next is first of next column with y= low: y difference could be big. 

This might result in a large delta_y for that step. 

So the idea is to alternate direction for the y coordinates:

Sort by x, and for each column of x, alternate the y-directions (ascending and descending), so then you can move in zigzag path, making those big jumps smaller.

Like:

Even x's sort y in ascending, then next x (odd) sort in descending, then even next x ascending etc.

This way, after the end of one column's y's, you move to the next column's starting point (either highest or lowest) which is closer.

Example:

After x=A, which was sorted by descending y: the last point in that column has the lowest y. The next column's x=A+1 has y's sorted in ascending: first point is the smallest y. So y difference would be (current y's minimal and previous minimal). 

Thus the delta Y between columns can be minimized. Similarly for other directions. 

This is called a "snake" pattern in grid traversal.

Thus this way, between different x columns, the y's between the end-to-start can be minimized.

So the total Manhattan distance would be:

steps inside x column contribute a summed manhattan distance of a small delta y (as they are sorted!)

steps between column: x steps (dx of 1) plus delta y between last y of previous column and first y of next column.

If you alternate direction for each x, then the delta y can be as low as 1 or 2?

At least, the worst case step between two columns would have delta_y of (max of previous's low and next's high). 

This might reduce the total distance between such steps.

tool: Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /testbed/src/scim2_filter_parser/transpilers/sql.py:
     1	"""
     2	The logic in this module builds a portion of a WHERE SQL
     3	clause based on a SCIM filter.
     4	"""
     5	import ast
     6	import string
     7	import collections
     8	
     9	from .. import ast as scim2ast
    10	
    11	AttrPath = collections.namedtuple('AttrPath', ['attr_name', 'sub_attr', 'uri'])
    12	
    13	
    14	class Transpiler(ast.NodeTransformer):
    15	    """
    16	    Transpile a SCIM AST into a SQL WHERE clause (not including the "WHERE" keyword)
    17	    """
    18	    binary_op_by_scim_op = {
    19	        'eq': '=',
    20	        'ne': '!=',
    21	        'co': 'LIKE',
    22	        'sw': 'LIKE',
    23	        'ew': 'LIKE',
    24	        'pr': 'IS NOT NULL',
    25	        'gt': '>',
    26	        'ge': '>=',
    27	        'lt': '<',
    28	        'le': '<=',
    29	    }
    30	
    31	    matching_op_by_scim_op = {
    32	        'co': ('%', '%'),
    33	        'sw': ('', '%'),
    34	        'ew': ('%', ''),
    35	    }
    36	
    37	    def __init__(self, attr_map, *args, **kwargs):
    38	        super().__init__(*args, **kwargs)
    39	
    40	        self.attr_map = attr_map
    41	        self.params = {}
    42	        self.attr_paths = []
    43	
    44	    def transpile(self, ast) -> (str, dict):
    45	        sql = self.visit(ast)
    46	
    47	        return sql, self.params
    48	
    49	    def visit_Filter(self, node):
    50	        if node.namespace:
    51	            # push the namespace from value path down the tree
    52	            if isinstance(node.expr, scim2ast.Filter):
    53	                node.expr = scim2ast.Filter(node.expr.expr, node.expr.negated, node.namespace)
    54	            elif isinstance(node.expr, scim2ast.LogExpr):
    55	                expr1 = scim2ast.Filter(node.expr.expr1.expr, node.expr.expr1.negated, node.namespace)
    56	                expr2 = scim2ast.Filter(node.expr.expr2.expr, node.expr.expr2.negated, node.namespace)
    57	                node.expr = scim2ast.LogExpr(node.expr.op, expr1, expr2)
    58	            elif isinstance(node.expr, scim2ast.AttrExpr):
    59	                # namespace takes place of previous attr_name in attr_path
    60	                sub_attr = scim2ast.SubAttr(node.expr.attr_path.attr_name)
    61	                attr_path = scim2ast.AttrPath(node.namespace.attr_name, sub_attr, node.expr.attr_path.uri)
    62	                node.expr = scim2ast.AttrExpr(node.expr.value, attr_path, node.expr.comp_value)
    63	            else:
    64	                raise NotImplementedError(f'Node {node} can not pass on namespace')
    65	
    66	        expr = self.visit(node.expr)
    67	
    68	        if expr and node.negated:
    69	            expr = f'NOT ({expr})'
    70	
    71	        return expr
    72	
    73	    def visit_LogExpr(self, node):
    74	        expr1 = self.visit(node.expr1)
    75	        expr2 = self.visit(node.expr2)
    76	        op = node.op.upper()
    77	
    78	        if expr1 and expr2:
    79	            return f'({expr1}) {op} ({expr2})'
    80	        elif expr1:
    81	            return expr1
    82	        elif expr2:
